metrics = {'T_median': 10.396666666666667, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 0.334957310724379, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 1765, 'T_mean': 12.907714654177628, 'T_median': 10.396666666666667, 'T_min': 4, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'licking': 0.9588318123909042, 'position': 0.25723790510097916, 'running_speed': 0.25029903335979...y': {'T_max': 238, 'T_mean': 32.62721547677432, 'T_median': 31.0387811634349, 'T_min': 11, ...}, 'device': 'cuda', ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"

description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. Directly use running speed and discretize into categories.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *running_speed*?\n\ni. The speeds are split into four bins holding a quarter of the frames each. The split is on rank rather than on a threshold, and is taken over the frames the dataset keeps, once per session, so the frames of a dropped trial do not move the boundaries.\n\nii. The split, and where it is applied:\n```python\ndef quartiles(values):\n    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n```\n```python\nkept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\nspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\nspeed[window]\n```\n\niii. Up to a third of the frames of a session sit at exactly zero speed, so we take a rank ordering and divide into 4 equal bins.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *running_speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_RunSpeed` gives one speed per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. Measured over the whole dataset even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample and a\n    full run agree on which trials to keep.\n\n    records: list of (subject, session_id, beh_file, bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    # a quarter or more of the frames can sit at exactly zero speed, and no threshold can\n    # cut a tie like that into equal parts, so the split is on rank rather than on value\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n\n\n# --- neural ---\n\ndef load_spikes(session_id, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Deconvolved traces of the neurons of one session, and the visual area of each.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011YW8SbM4z91cyLHS7x7kXV","type":"tool_result","content":"Similar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as ‘frozen’ crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as ‘leaf’ and ‘circle’, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (‘rock’ and ‘bricks’; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli ‘leaf2’ and ‘circle2’, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (‘leaf3’) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\n\nMice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\n\nBefore and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\n\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \n\nMethods\n\nWe performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s × camK2a-tTa mice.\n\nThe mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\n\nVisual stimuli\n\nWe showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270° of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4 m long, with 2 m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6 cm s−1, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60 cm s−1) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\n\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0° and 45°) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (‘unsupervised gratings’ and VRg mice).\n\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\n\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\n\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\n\nBehavioural training\n\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5–1 h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1 h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality + reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\n\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality + reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2 m and 3 m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2–3 m.\n\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5 m and 3.5 m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5 m and 3.5 m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75 s (ref. 65). These are non-negative deconvolved fluorescence traces.\"\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No normalization, no dF/F, no deconvolution, no z-scoring, no position interpolation — the deconvolved traces are used as they are. The only transformation is temporal: within each trial, frames are grouped into non-overlapping 1 s bins measured from the first corridor frame, and the mean over the frames in each bin is taken per neuron. The result is stored as `float32` with shape (n_neurons, n_bins). If a bin happens to contain no frame, the previous bin's whole population vector is copied forward (zeros for the first bin). I measured this case to be very rare — 13 of 98,042 bins (0.013%) over 20 sessions — so it is a fabrication in principle but negligible in practice. The `float32` choice (against the reference's `float16`) is the main reason the output is 94.72 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\n        N=np.empty((spk.shape[0],nb),np.float32); P=np.empty(nb,np.float32); V=np.empty(nb,np.float32)\n        L=np.zeros(nb,np.int16)\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 17: \"The shown downstream code computes d-prime from raw corridor frames and does not quality-filter neurons beyond Suite2p cell classification already applied upstream. It normalizes only for specific figures; the conversion should retain deconvolved traces rather than applying that figure-specific normalization.\" Step 22/23 give the reason for averaging: \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin... This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. It is not filtered at all. Every neuron in every plane is kept — 4,691,034 in total, against the reference's 4,105,393 (ratio 1.14, which failed the neuron-count check). The AI does assign a region index per neuron, but the mapping it uses is wrong. The repository's `utils.neu_area_ID` maps `iarea == 8 → V1`, `{0,1,2,9} → mHV`, `{5,6} → lHV`, `{3,4} → aHV`. The AI's code claims to follow that function but maps `iarea == 1 → V1`, `{2,3} → medial`, `{4,5} → anterior`, `{6,7} → lateral`, everything else → \"unassigned\". Since `iarea == 8` is the single largest group (26,069 / 58,224 = 45% for `DR10_2022_07_12`), real V1 is entirely labelled \"unassigned\": the run reported only 147,071 \"V1\" neurons and ~3.05 M (65%) \"unassigned\". There is also a silent fallback that returns all zeros if the retinotopy file is missing and zero-pads if the lengths disagree.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef region_indices(sid,n):\n    p=sid.rsplit('_',1)[0]\n    f=os.path.join(ROOT,'retinotopy',p+'_trans.npz')\n    if not os.path.exists(f): return np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16)\n    a=np.asarray(np.load(f)['iarea']).ravel()\n    # Repository neu_area_ID: 1=V1, 2=medial, 3=anterior, 4=lateral; 0=unassigned.\n    out=np.zeros(len(a),dtype=np.int16)\n    out[a==1]=1; out[np.isin(a,[2,3])]=2; out[np.isin(a,[4,5])]=3; out[np.isin(a,[6,7])]=4\n    if len(out)!=n:\n        # Retinotopy corresponds to concatenated planes; guard malformed metadata.\n        z=np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16); z[:min(n,len(out))]=out[:min(n,len(out))]; out=z\n    return out\n```\n```python\n 'brain_regions':['unassigned','V1','medial visual areas','anterior visual areas','lateral visual areas'],'brain_region_idx':bridx,\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Suite2p cell classification is the supplied curation; no post-hoc selectivity filter.\" Step 21 shows the agent *considered* area-based exclusion purely for size reasons — \"This determines whether excluding `iarea==0` unassigned/non-visual ROIs—as paper area-specific analyses may do—makes the dataset tractable\" — and step 22, \"If all neurons are consumed, use the paper's four named visual regions and exclude unassigned ROIs\", but step 23 settled on \"We will retain all Suite2p-classified neurons\". Step 43 identified only the filename bug (\"every neuron was labeled unassigned because the retinotopy filename was constructed without the day component\"), not the code-value mapping; step 56 then reports the wrong counts as a success: \"populated retinotopic region assignments: 147,071 V1, 708,036 medial, 441,492 anterior, 341,596 lateral, plus unassigned neurons.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry / trial start. The AI sets the trial origin `t0` to the timestamp of the *first corridor frame* of that trial (rather than interpolating the fractional `StartFr`), and bins forward from there, so bin 0 begins at corridor entry. Trials are left at their natural, variable length; nothing is padded or truncated to a common window. The metadata reports `temporal_alignment_event = 'trial start / corridor entry'`, `off_start = 0.0`, `off_end = None`. This is conceptually the same alignment as the reference; the only difference is the sub-frame offset between the first corridor frame and the fractional `StartFr`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'temporal_alignment_event':'trial start / corridor entry','off_start':0.0,'off_end':None,\n```\n\niii. Step 20: \"the decoder explicitly requires temporal alignment\"; step 22: \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed, so a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit\". Step 23: \"bins frames in 1-second bins from trial/corridor entry through corridor exit\".\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Yes, rebinning is applied. The native imaging rate is ~3.17 Hz (315 ms per frame), and the AI averages roughly three native frames into each non-overlapping 1000 ms bin, indexed by `floor(rel / 1 s)` from corridor entry. `metadata['time_bin_size']` is 1000.0 ms and `metadata['temporal_binning']` says \"non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit\". The consequence is that the median trial is 10.4 bins long against the reference's 31.0 native frames (ratio 0.33), which failed the median-trial-length check. The stated motivation — tractability — is not really borne out: the 3× reduction was offset by keeping all 4.69 M neurons in `float32`, and the output is still 94.72 GB, comparable to a native-resolution `float16` conversion.\n\nii.\n```python\nROOT='/app/data'; BIN_S=1.0\n```\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size':1000.0, ... 'source_frame_rate_hz':'approximately 3.2, variable','temporal_binning':'non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit',\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Imaging timestamps are MATLAB serial-day values: median spacing × 86,400 is ~0.315 s, i.e. ~3.18 Hz.\" Step 21–23 give the justification: \"Fully materializing every neuron and native frame into a pickle would remain hundreds of GB and make decoder training impractical\"; \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin. This reduces size by about 3× only and may remain large\"; \"We will use 1-second non-overlapping temporal bins aligned to corridor entry, averaging deconvolved traces. This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr` (the fractional imaging-frame index of the cue on each trial) and `ft` (the MATLAB datenum timestamp of every imaging frame). No session has a non-finite `SoundFr` (checked across all 142 session entries), so the NaN fallback never fires.\n\nii.\n```python\n    ntr=int(b['ntrials']); trialstim=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); sound=np.asarray(b['SoundFr']); lick=np.asarray(b['LickFr'])\n```\n```python\n        cue_rel=(np.interp(sound[tr],np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0) if tr<len(sound) and np.isfinite(sound[tr]) else np.nan\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Trial event fields such as `StartFr`, `SoundFr`, and `EndFr` are fractional imaging-frame indices, while `ft_trInd` directly assigns frames to trials.\" Code comment: \"Cue event is a fractional global imaging-frame index.\"\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. Frame timestamps are converted from MATLAB datenums to elapsed seconds (`(ft - ft[0]) * 86400`). The fractional cue frame is interpolated onto that time axis and expressed relative to the trial origin, giving `cue_rel`. The input is then `cue_rel − elapsed`, where `elapsed` is the bin-centre time, so the value is **positive before the cue and negative after** — the same sign convention as the reference. It is stored as `float32`. Because no duration filter is applied, this variable reaches −1762.8 s in the delivered data.\n\nii.\n```python\n    tsec=(ft-ft[0])*86400.0\n```\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        tcue=(cue_rel-elapsed).astype(np.float32) if np.isfinite(cue_rel) else np.full(nb,np.nan,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Step 12: \"Frame timestamps are not in seconds—the naïve reciprocal-difference calculation produced ~274 kHz—so they likely use MATLAB day units or another scale and must be converted before temporal binning\", resolved in step 13. Step 23: \"creates continuous time-to-cue, day, elapsed time, and rewarded-corridor inputs\".\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is defined on exactly the same 1 s bin grid as the neural data for that trial: the same `t0`, the same `nb`, and bin centres `(j + 0.5) s`. Since the cue time is placed on the same seconds axis derived from `ft`, the two streams share one clock.\n\nii.\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1)\n```\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        tcue=(cue_rel-elapsed).astype(np.float32) ...\n        inp=np.vstack([tcue,day,elapsed,rewarded]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Implicit in step 22/23: everything is put on the common \"1-second temporal bins aligned to corridor entry\" grid, so no separate alignment step is needed.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. From `datexp` in the `Imaging_Exp_info` entry for the session (kept in `rowmap`), and from the mouse name used to find that mouse's earliest recorded date. No other field is used.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef date_ordinal(date):\n    return float(np.datetime64(date.replace('_','-'),'D').astype(int))\n```\n```python\nsubject_day0={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    mouse=s.split('_')[0]; d=date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])\n    subject_day0[mouse]=min(subject_day0.get(mouse,d),d)\n```\n\niii. Step 19: \"need make final choices for rewarded-corridor identification, training-day values, trial inclusion, and practical time resampling. These should be derived directly from metadata and methods rather than guessed.\"\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Day of training*?\n\ni. The date string `YYYY_MM_DD` is converted to a day ordinal; the value is `ordinal − (earliest ordinal for that mouse) + 1`, i.e. **calendar days elapsed** since the mouse's first included imaging session, starting at 1. It is broadcast as a constant across all bins of every trial in that session, as `float32`. The delivered range is 1–93. The reference instead counts *recorded sessions* per mouse, giving 0–7; the two encode the same ordering but on very different scales, and the AI's version is dominated by the multi-week gaps between pre- and post-learning blocks. This difference is what produced the large `input_range_error_day_of_training` in the metrics.\n\nii.\n```python\n        day=np.full(nb,date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])-subject_day0[s.split('_')[0]]+1.0,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. This was a deliberate second-pass correction. Step 43: \"training day should be relative to each subject's first imaging date rather than an absolute date ordinal\"; step 44: \"The script was patched with the corrected visual-corridor mask, 4 m position scale, relative training days, and retinotopy filename.\" (The first run had used the raw absolute date ordinal, which was clearly wrong.)\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since trial start* derived from?\n\ni. Strictly speaking, only the bin index — the value is constructed analytically from the bin grid rather than from a raw field. Step 23: \"creates continuous time-to-cue, day, elapsed time, and rewarded-corridor inputs\". The agent treated elapsed time as a property of the bin grid it had just defined.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since trial start*?\n\ni. The trial origin is the timestamp of the first corridor frame; the input is the bin-centre time, `(j + 0.5) × 1 s`, so it starts at 0.5 s and increases in exact 1 s steps. It is always non-negative (there are no pre-trial bins) and is stored as `float32`. Because no duration filter is applied, it reaches 1764.5 s on the stalled trials. The reference instead takes each frame's true timestamp minus the interpolated `StartFr`, which is the same quantity up to the sub-frame origin offset and to within-bin jitter.\n\nii.\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        ...\n        inp=np.vstack([tcue,day,elapsed,rewarded]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Implicit in the single shared bin grid (step 22/23).\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Reward availability* derived from?\n\ni. From `TrialStim` (the per-trial stimulus label) and `RewardFr` (the frame at which reward was delivered, NaN when none). The AI does **not** use `isRew`, the boolean per-trial field that directly states whether the trial was run in the rewarded corridor, even though `isRew` is present in every one of the 142 session entries.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_stim={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    b=beh[s]; st=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); rw=np.asarray(b['RewardFr'])\n    counts={x:int(np.isfinite(rw[st==x]).sum()) for x in np.unique(st)}\n    reward_stim[s]=max(counts,key=counts.get) if counts and max(counts.values())>0 else None\n```\n\niii. Per session, the AI counts, for each distinct `TrialStim` value, how many of its trials have a finite `RewardFr`, and takes the arg-max as \"the rewarded stimulus\" (None if no reward was ever delivered — unsupervised and naive sessions). A trial is then flagged 1 if its `TrialStim` equals that stimulus, else 0, and the flag is broadcast across all bins as `float32`. I checked this reconstruction against `isRew` over all 63,177 trials in the behavior files: it disagrees on 155 trials (0.25%). So it is close to, but not identical to, the ground-truth field, and it inherits any weakness of `TrialStim` (see 7-a).\n\nii.\n```python\n        rewarded=np.full(nb,int(reward_stim[s] is not None and str(trialstim[tr])==reward_stim[s]),np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Visual stimulus category* derived from?\n\ni. From `TrialStim`. This is the wrong field. `TrialStim` is an *anonymised/remapped* analysis label, not the physical texture: in `TX119_2023_12_12_1`, for instance, wall texture `rock1` is labelled `circle1`, `rock2` → `circle2`, `wood1` → `leaf1`, `wood2` → `leaf2`, and `wood5` → the literal placeholder string `'stimulus_of_trial'`. Across the dataset `TrialStim` takes 8 values (`circle1, circle2, leaf1, leaf1_swap1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2, leaf3, stimulus_of_trial`), and 8,594 of 63,177 trials (13.6%) carry the placeholder; 64 of the 142 session entries are partially masked. The field that actually names the texture is `WallName` (15 values, resolving to the four base textures circle / leaf / rock / wood), which the reference uses.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_names=sorted({str(x) for s in sessions for x in np.unique(beh[s]['TrialStim'])}); stim_id={x:i for i,x in enumerate(stim_names)}\n```\n```python\n        cat=np.full(nb,stim_id[str(trialstim[tr])],np.int16)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. A single global vocabulary is built as the sorted set of all `TrialStim` strings seen across all 89 sessions, giving 8 classes, and each trial's label is the index into that list, broadcast across all of its bins as `int16`. The recorded metrics confirm 8 classes against the reference's 4, and the class-fraction comparison for this variable failed. The practical damage is twofold: rock/wood trials are labelled as circle/leaf, and 13.6% of trials are collapsed into a meaningless placeholder class that mixes several distinct textures.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_names=sorted({str(x) for s in sessions for x in np.unique(beh[s]['TrialStim'])}); stim_id={x:i for i,x in enumerate(stim_names)}\n```\n```python\n        cat=np.full(nb,stim_id[str(trialstim[tr])],np.int16)\n```\n```python\n 'output_values':[stim_names,['not licking','licking'],['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m'],['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']],\n```\n\niii. No explicit justification for the granularity is given. The global vocabulary is a deliberate choice so that class indices are comparable across sessions. The agent read the paper's stimulus description (step 8: \"For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as 'leaf' and 'circle', even though other visual stimuli were also used ('rock' and 'bricks')\" appears in the methods it summarised) but did not reconcile that with the field it chose.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Licking* derived from?\n\ni. From `LickFr`, the fractional imaging-frame index of every lick in the session, together with `ft` for the time conversion. Step 11 identified \"lick frames\" as directly available in the behavior dict.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. Non-finite lick frames are dropped. The remaining fractional frame indices are interpolated onto the seconds axis, expressed relative to the trial origin `t0`, floored to a bin index, restricted to `[0, nb)`, and the corresponding bins are set to 1; all other bins are 0. So a bin is 1 if at least one lick falls in it. Stored as `int16`, two classes. One small wrinkle: `np.interp` *clamps* rather than drops lick frames beyond the last imaged frame, mapping them to the final frame time, whereas the reference discards them — this can add at most a spurious lick to the last trial of a session. The delivered class fractions are within tolerance of the reference (fraction error 0.037).\n\nii.\n```python\n        lf=lick[np.isfinite(lick)] if lick.size else lick\n        if lf.size:\n            lt=np.interp(lf,np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0; lb=(lt/BIN_S).astype(int); lb=lb[(lb>=0)&(lb<nb)]; L[np.unique(lb)]=1\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Any lick in a temporal bin -> binary licking.\" Step 22: the 1 s temporal binning was chosen specifically because \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed\".\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Through the same trial origin `t0` and the same 1 s bin grid of length `nb` as the neural array, with licks assigned to bins by their interpolated time. Step 43: \"stored sessions contain a 4 m visual corridor plus 2 m gray space (`Texture_Length=40`, `Corridor_Length=60`), so only `ft_CorrSpc` should be used for the requested four 1 m corridor bins\". Note the module docstring was never updated and still says \"Stored corridor coordinates span 60 units = 4 m\", which contradicts both the data and the implemented code.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Within each 1 s bin, the mean position over the frames in that bin is taken (`np.nanmean`), producing a continuous `float32` value per bin; that mean is then discretised. Empty bins carry the previous bin's value forward (0 for the first bin). The reference discretises the raw per-frame position instead, but since position increases monotonically within a traversal and a bin spans ~3 frames, the two agree closely — the recorded position class-fraction error is 0.018, well inside tolerance.\n\nii.\n```python\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 22/23: behavior is averaged in the same 1 s bins as the neural data.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `clip(int(P / Texture_Length × 4), 0, 3)` — a linear map of the 0–40 unit textured corridor onto four equal 1 m bins, labelled `['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m']`. With `Texture_Length = 40` this is arithmetically identical to the reference's `clip(ft_Pos // 10, 0, 3)`. The grey space is excluded upstream by the `ft_CorrSpc` mask, so the clip almost never binds. The recorded output has exactly 4 classes with fractions matching the reference.\n\nii.\n```python\n        # Linear mapping required by task: 4 equal 1 m bins over each session corridor.\n        pcat=np.clip((P/float(b['Texture_Length'])*4).astype(int),0,3).astype(np.int16)\n```\n```python\n'position_mapping':'Visual-corridor coordinates (ft_CorrSpc; Texture_Length=40 units) mapped to four equal 1 m bins; gray space excluded',\n```\n\niii. Step 16 flagged the ambiguity — \"The notebook comment says 60 bins over a 6 m corridor, conflicting with the task's explicit 4 equal 1 m bins and methods' apparent 4 m wording\" — and step 43 resolved it correctly against `Texture_Length`. Step 54: \"reduced by excluding the 2 m gray-space segment while preserving the 4 m visual corridor required by the decoder task.\"\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Position in corridor* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_Pos` is sampled per imaging frame, and the per-bin mean is computed in the same loop, over the same frame indices `jj`, as the per-bin neural mean. So position and neural activity share the bin grid exactly.\n\nii.\n```python\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n```\n\niii. First, a pre-pass over all 89 sessions concatenates the per-frame speeds of all frames that will be kept (corridor frames with finite position/speed/trial index) and computes the 25/50/75% quantile edges *globally*, i.e. pooled across sessions. Second, in the main loop, the per-bin mean speed is computed alongside the per-bin mean neural activity and position, and that bin-averaged speed is what gets discretised. The reference instead computes the split per session and on the raw per-frame speeds.\n\nii.\n```python\nspeeds=[]\nfor s in sessions:\n    b=beh[s]; tri=np.asarray(b['ft_trInd']); pos=np.asarray(b['ft_Pos']); v=np.asarray(b['ft_RunSpeed'])\n    ok=np.isfinite(tri)&np.isfinite(pos)&np.isfinite(v)&np.asarray(b['ft_CorrSpc'],dtype=bool)&(pos>=0)&(pos<float(b['Texture_Length']))\n    speeds.append(v[ok].astype(np.float32))\nq=np.quantile(np.concatenate(speeds),[.25,.5,.75]).astype(np.float32); del speeds\n```\n```python\n            if jj.size: ... Step 23: \"To avoid loading all sessions twice for speed quartiles, first derive global quartiles from framewise speed over usable trial frames.\" The comment in the code reads \"Global speed quartiles from the same valid corridor frames included below.\"\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `np.digitize(V, q, right=False)` against the three global quantile edges, labelled `['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']`. This does **not** produce four bins of 25% of the data, which is what the task asks for. Roughly 27.5% of corridor frames have a speed of exactly 0 (the VR is stationary below the 6 cm/s running threshold), so the 25th-percentile edge is `q[0] = 0.0`; with `right=False`, `digitize(0.0, [0.0, ...])` returns 1, so the entire zero-speed mass lands in class 1 and class 0 is left with only the (empty) strictly-negative range. On a 25-session sample I measured the resulting frame-level split as 9.3% / 40.7% / 25.0% / 25.0%. The delivered file shows the same problem: `output_fraction_error_running_speed = 0.32`, the only output class-fraction check that failed besides the stimulus. Averaging speeds within a bin before applying frame-level edges shifts the split further.\n\nii.\n```python\nq=np.quantile(np.concatenate(speeds),[.25,.5,.75]).astype(np.float32); del speeds\n```\n```python\n        vcat=np.digitize(V,q,right=False).astype(np.int16)\n```\n```python\n 'output_values':[stim_names,['not licking','licking'],['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m'],['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']],\n```\n\niii. It never checked the resulting class balance, and never noticed the tie at zero. Several small guards, most of which never actually fire on this dataset:\n- Behavior can run past the imaging, so all frame-wise streams are truncated to `nfr = min(n_spike_frames, n_behavior_frames)` — the same cut the reference makes.\n- Frames with non-finite `ft_trInd`, `ft_Pos` or out-of-range position are excluded from trials (a no-op here: `ft_CorrSpc` already implies `0 <= ft_Pos < 40`).\n- Non-finite `LickFr` entries are dropped; licks outside the trial window are dropped (but licks past the last imaged frame are clamped by `np.interp` rather than dropped).\n- A trial with a non-finite `SoundFr` would get an all-NaN cue input; no session has one, so this never fires.\n- Within-bin averages use `np.nanmean`; a bin with no frames copies the previous bin forward for neural, position and speed (or zeros at bin 0). I measured this at 13 of 98,042 bins over 20 sessions (0.013%).\n- A missing retinotopy file silently returns all-zero region indices, and a length mismatch between `iarea` and the neuron count silently zero-pads. Neither condition occurs (`iarea` length equals the concatenated neuron count).\n- Sessions listed under several experiment types are deduplicated; sessions without a spike file are skipped; sessions ending with fewer than 2 usable trials are skipped (none are).\nThere is no handling of the *large* data problem in this dataset — the stalled multi-minute \"traversals\" (see 1-e).\n\nii.\n```python\n    nfr=min(spk.shape[1],len(b['ft_trInd']))\n```\n```python\n        ix=np.where((tri==tr)&np.asarray(b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr],dtype=bool)&np.isfinite(pos)&(pos>=0)&(pos<float(b['Texture_Length'])))[0]\n        if ix.size<2: continue\n```\n```python\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n```python\n    if not os.path.exists(f): return np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16)\n    ...\n    if len(out)!=n:\n        z=np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16); z[:min(n,len(out))]=out[:min(n,len(out))]; out=z\n```\n\niii. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Three things dominate, in rough order:\n1. Reading the spike files — 89 pickled `.npy` object files totalling ~405 GB (the agent measured \"374 GB of source activity\" for the subset it had enumerated at the time). What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Four, the first of which is the significant one:\n1. **The per-bin averaging loop** (`for j in range(nb)`). Because the trial's frames are contiguous and sorted, the whole per-bin mean could be done in one call with `np.add.reduceat(spk[:, ix], starts, axis=1)` divided by the bin counts, or as a single matrix multiply against a sparse bin-indicator matrix. As written it performs one fancy-index and one reduction per bin over all ~50,000 neurons, plus `ix[bins==j]`, which itself rescans the whole trial index once per bin (an O(n_bins × n_frames) scan hidden inside the loop).\n2. **The per-trial frame search** (`np.where((tri==tr) & ...)` inside `for tr in ...`), which rescans the entire session's frame index once per trial — O(n_trials × n_frames). Grouping all frames by `ft_trInd` in one pass (e.g. `np.argsort` or `np.bincount`-based splitting) would make this a single sweep. The reference has the same pattern and calls it out as negligible; here it is amplified because the mask is rebuilt from `b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr]` each time.\n3. **`subjects.index(x)`** inside a list comprehension over sessions — O(n_sessions × n_subjects); trivial in absolute terms.\n4. The three separate full passes over `sessions` (speed quartiles, reward inference, conversion) could be folded into fewer passes.\n\nii.\n```python\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n```\n```python\n    for tr in range(min(ntr,len(trialstim))):\n        ix=np.where((tri==tr)&np.asarray(b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr],dtype=bool)&np.isfinite(pos)&(pos>=0)&(pos<float(b['Texture_Length'])))[0]\n```\n```python\nsubjects=sorted(set(subj)); subject_idx=np.array([subjects.index(x) for x in subj],dtype=np.int16)\n```\n\niii. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI reads three of the four directories under `/app/data`. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is loaded first as the master index; it is a dict keyed by experiment type, each value a list of recording entries. For every experiment type the matching `beh/Beh_<type>.npy` is loaded once and each of its per-session behavior dicts is stored in a flat dict `beh[base_session_id]`. Spike files are discovered by globbing `spk/*_neural_data.npy` rather than being constructed from the index, and the usable set of sessions is the intersection of the behavior keys and the spike-file keys (89 sessions). Retinotopy (`retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`) is loaded lazily, once per session, at the end of that session's processing. `data/process_data` is never read. These are non-negative deconvolved fluorescence traces.\"\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No normalization, no dF/F, no deconvolution, no z-scoring, no position interpolation — the deconvolved traces are used as they are. The only transformation is temporal: within each trial, frames are grouped into non-overlapping 1 s bins measured from the first corridor frame, and the mean over the frames in each bin is taken per neuron. The result is stored as `float32` with shape (n_neurons, n_bins). If a bin happens to contain no frame, the previous bin's whole population vector is copied forward (zeros for the first bin). I measured this case to be very rare — 13 of 98,042 bins (0.013%) over 20 sessions — so it is a fabrication in principle but negligible in practice. The `float32` choice (against the reference's `float16`) is the main reason the output is 94.72 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\n        N=np.empty((spk.shape[0],nb),np.float32); P=np.empty(nb,np.float32); V=np.empty(nb,np.float32)\n        L=np.zeros(nb,np.int16)\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 17: \"The shown downstream code computes d-prime from raw corridor frames and does not quality-filter neurons beyond Suite2p cell classification already applied upstream. It normalizes only for specific figures; the conversion should retain deconvolved traces rather than applying that figure-specific normalization.\" Step 22/23 give the reason for averaging: \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin... This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. It is not filtered at all. Every neuron in every plane is kept — 4,691,034 in total, against the reference's 4,105,393 (ratio 1.14, which failed the neuron-count check). The AI does assign a region index per neuron, but the mapping it uses is wrong. The repository's `utils.neu_area_ID` maps `iarea == 8 → V1`, `{0,1,2,9} → mHV`, `{5,6} → lHV`, `{3,4} → aHV`. The AI's code claims to follow that function but maps `iarea == 1 → V1`, `{2,3} → medial`, `{4,5} → anterior`, `{6,7} → lateral`, everything else → \"unassigned\". Since `iarea == 8` is the single largest group (26,069 / 58,224 = 45% for `DR10_2022_07_12`), real V1 is entirely labelled \"unassigned\": the run reported only 147,071 \"V1\" neurons and ~3.05 M (65%) \"unassigned\". There is also a silent fallback that returns all zeros if the retinotopy file is missing and zero-pads if the lengths disagree.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef region_indices(sid,n):\n    p=sid.rsplit('_',1)[0]\n    f=os.path.join(ROOT,'retinotopy',p+'_trans.npz')\n    if not os.path.exists(f): return np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16)\n    a=np.asarray(np.load(f)['iarea']).ravel()\n    # Repository neu_area_ID: 1=V1, 2=medial, 3=anterior, 4=lateral; 0=unassigned.\n    out=np.zeros(len(a),dtype=np.int16)\n    out[a==1]=1; out[np.isin(a,[2,3])]=2; out[np.isin(a,[4,5])]=3; out[np.isin(a,[6,7])]=4\n    if len(out)!=n:\n        # Retinotopy corresponds to concatenated planes; guard malformed metadata.\n        z=np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16); z[:min(n,len(out))]=out[:min(n,len(out))]; out=z\n    return out\n```\n```python\n 'brain_regions':['unassigned','V1','medial visual areas','anterior visual areas','lateral visual areas'],'brain_region_idx':bridx,\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Suite2p cell classification is the supplied curation; no post-hoc selectivity filter.\" Step 21 shows the agent *considered* area-based exclusion purely for size reasons — \"This determines whether excluding `iarea==0` unassigned/non-visual ROIs—as paper area-specific analyses may do—makes the dataset tractable\" — and step 22, \"If all neurons are consumed, use the paper's four named visual regions and exclude unassigned ROIs\", but step 23 settled on \"We will retain all Suite2p-classified neurons\". Step 43 identified only the filename bug (\"every neuron was labeled unassigned because the retinotopy filename was constructed without the day component\"), not the code-value mapping; step 56 then reports the wrong counts as a success: \"populated retinotopic region assignments: 147,071 V1, 708,036 medial, 441,492 anterior, 341,596 lateral, plus unassigned neurons.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry / trial start. The AI sets the trial origin `t0` to the timestamp of the *first corridor frame* of that trial (rather than interpolating the fractional `StartFr`), and bins forward from there, so bin 0 begins at corridor entry. Trials are left at their natural, variable length; nothing is padded or truncated to a common window. The metadata reports `temporal_alignment_event = 'trial start / corridor entry'`, `off_start = 0.0`, `off_end = None`. This is conceptually the same alignment as the reference; the only difference is the sub-frame offset between the first corridor frame and the fractional `StartFr`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'temporal_alignment_event':'trial start / corridor entry','off_start':0.0,'off_end':None,\n```\n\niii. Step 20: \"the decoder explicitly requires temporal alignment\"; step 22: \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed, so a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit\". Step 23: \"bins frames in 1-second bins from trial/corridor entry through corridor exit\".\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Yes, rebinning is applied. The native imaging rate is ~3.17 Hz (315 ms per frame), and the AI averages roughly three native frames into each non-overlapping 1000 ms bin, indexed by `floor(rel / 1 s)` from corridor entry. `metadata['time_bin_size']` is 1000.0 ms and `metadata['temporal_binning']` says \"non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit\". The consequence is that the median trial is 10.4 bins long against the reference's 31.0 native frames (ratio 0.33), which failed the median-trial-length check. The stated motivation — tractability — is not really borne out: the 3× reduction was offset by keeping all 4.69 M neurons in `float32`, and the output is still 94.72 GB, comparable to a native-resolution `float16` conversion.\n\nii.\n```python\nROOT='/app/data'; BIN_S=1.0\n```\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size':1000.0, ... 'source_frame_rate_hz':'approximately 3.2, variable','temporal_binning':'non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit',\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Imaging timestamps are MATLAB serial-day values: median spacing × 86,400 is ~0.315 s, i.e. ~3.18 Hz.\" Step 21–23 give the justification: \"Fully materializing every neuron and native frame into a pickle would remain hundreds of GB and make decoder training impractical\"; \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin. This reduces size by about 3× only and may remain large\"; \"We will use 1-second non-overlapping temporal bins aligned to corridor entry, averaging deconvolved traces. This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr` (the fractional imaging-frame index of the cue on each trial) and `ft` (the MATLAB datenum timestamp of every imaging frame). No session has a non-finite `SoundFr` (checked across all 142 session entries), so the NaN fallback never fires.\n\nii.\n```python\n    ntr=int(b['ntrials']); trialstim=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); sound=np.asarray(b['SoundFr']); lick=np.asarray(b['LickFr'])\n```\n```python\n        cue_rel=(np.interp(sound[tr],np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0) if tr<len(sound) and np.isfinite(sound[tr]) else np.nan\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Trial event fields such as `StartFr`, `SoundFr`, and `EndFr` are fractional imaging-frame indices, while `ft_trInd` directly assigns frames to trials.\" Code comment: \"Cue event is a fractional global imaging-frame index.\"\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. Frame timestamps are converted from MATLAB datenums to elapsed seconds (`(ft - ft[0]) * 86400`). The fractional cue frame is interpolated onto that time axis and expressed relative to the trial origin, giving `cue_rel`. The input is then `cue_rel − elapsed`, where `elapsed` is the bin-centre time, so the value is **positive before the cue and negative after** — the same sign convention as the reference. It is stored as `float32`. Because no duration filter is applied, this variable reaches −1762.8 s in the delivered data.\n\nii.\n```python\n    tsec=(ft-ft[0])*86400.0\n```\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        tcue=(cue_rel-elapsed).astype(np.float32) if np.isfinite(cue_rel) else np.full(nb,np.nan,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Step 12: \"Frame timestamps are not in seconds—the naïve reciprocal-difference calculation produced ~274 kHz—so they likely use MATLAB day units or another scale and must be converted before temporal binning\", resolved in step 13. Step 23: \"creates continuous time-to-cue, day, elapsed time, and rewarded-corridor inputs\".\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is defined on exactly the same 1 s bin grid as the neural data for that trial: the same `t0`, the same `nb`, and bin centres `(j + 0.5) s`. Since the cue time is placed on the same seconds axis derived from `ft`, the two streams share one clock.\n\nii.\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1)\n```\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        tcue=(cue_rel-elapsed).astype(np.float32) ...\n        inp=np.vstack([tcue,day,elapsed,rewarded]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. From `datexp` in the `Imaging_Exp_info` entry for the session (kept in `rowmap`), and from the mouse name used to find that mouse's earliest recorded date. No other field is used.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef date_ordinal(date):\n    return float(np.datetime64(date.replace('_','-'),'D').astype(int))\n```\n```python\nsubject_day0={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    mouse=s.split('_')[0]; d=date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])\n    subject_day0[mouse]=min(subject_day0.get(mouse,d),d)\n```\n\niii. Step 19: \"need make final choices for rewarded-corridor identification, training-day values, trial inclusion, and practical time resampling. The date string `YYYY_MM_DD` is converted to a day ordinal; the value is `ordinal − (earliest ordinal for that mouse) + 1`, i.e. **calendar days elapsed** since the mouse's first included imaging session, starting at 1. It is broadcast as a constant across all bins of every trial in that session, as `float32`. The delivered range is 1–93. The reference instead counts *recorded sessions* per mouse, giving 0–7; the two encode the same ordering but on very different scales, and the AI's version is dominated by the multi-week gaps between pre- and post-learning blocks. This difference is what produced the large `input_range_error_day_of_training` in the metrics.\n\nii.\n```python\n        day=np.full(nb,date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])-subject_day0[s.split('_')[0]]+1.0,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since trial start*?\n\ni. The trial origin is the timestamp of the first corridor frame; the input is the bin-centre time, `(j + 0.5) × 1 s`, so it starts at 0.5 s and increases in exact 1 s steps. From `TrialStim` (the per-trial stimulus label) and `RewardFr` (the frame at which reward was delivered, NaN when none). The AI does **not** use `isRew`, the boolean per-trial field that directly states whether the trial was run in the rewarded corridor, even though `isRew` is present in every one of the 142 session entries.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_stim={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    b=beh[s]; st=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); rw=np.asarray(b['RewardFr'])\n    counts={x:int(np.isfinite(rw[st==x]).sum()) for x in np.unique(st)}\n    reward_stim[s]=max(counts,key=counts.get) if counts and max(counts.values())>0 else None\n```\n\niii. Per session, the AI counts, for each distinct `TrialStim` value, how many of its trials have a finite `RewardFr`, and takes the arg-max as \"the rewarded stimulus\" (None if no reward was ever delivered — unsupervised and naive sessions). A trial is then flagged 1 if its `TrialStim` equals that stimulus, else 0, and the flag is broadcast across all bins as `float32`. I checked this reconstruction against `isRew` over all 63,177 trials in the behavior files: it disagrees on 155 trials (0.25%). So it is close to, but not identical to, the ground-truth field, and it inherits any weakness of `TrialStim` (see 7-a).\n\nii.\n```python\n        rewarded=np.full(nb,int(reward_stim[s] is not None and str(trialstim[tr])==reward_stim[s]),np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Visual stimulus category* derived from?\n\ni. From `TrialStim`. This is the wrong field. `TrialStim` is an *anonymised/remapped* analysis label, not the physical texture: in `TX119_2023_12_12_1`, for instance, wall texture `rock1` is labelled `circle1`, `rock2` → `circle2`, `wood1` → `leaf1`, `wood2` → `leaf2`, and `wood5` → the literal placeholder string `'stimulus_of_trial'`. Across the dataset `TrialStim` takes 8 values (`circle1, circle2, leaf1, leaf1_swap1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2, leaf3, stimulus_of_trial`), and 8,594 of 63,177 trials (13.6%) carry the placeholder; 64 of the 142 session entries are partially masked. The field that actually names the texture is `WallName` (15 values, resolving to the four base textures circle / leaf / rock / wood), which the reference uses.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_names=sorted({str(x) for s in sessions for x in np.unique(beh[s]['TrialStim'])}); stim_id={x:i for i,x in enumerate(stim_names)}\n```\n```python\n        cat=np.full(nb,stim_id[str(trialstim[tr])],np.int16)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. A single global vocabulary is built as the sorted set of all `TrialStim` strings seen across all 89 sessions, giving 8 classes, and each trial's label is the index into that list, broadcast across all of its bins as `int16`. The agent read the paper's stimulus description (step 8: \"For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as 'leaf' and 'circle', even though other visual stimuli were also used ('rock' and 'bricks')\" appears in the methods it summarised) but did not reconcile that with the field it chose.\n\n## 8-a. Step 11 identified \"lick frames\" as directly available in the behavior dict.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. Non-finite lick frames are dropped. The remaining fractional frame indices are interpolated onto the seconds axis, expressed relative to the trial origin `t0`, floored to a bin index, restricted to `[0, nb)`, and the corresponding bins are set to 1; all other bins are 0. So a bin is 1 if at least one lick falls in it. Stored as `int16`, two classes. One small wrinkle: `np.interp` *clamps* rather than drops lick frames beyond the last imaged frame, mapping them to the final frame time, whereas the reference discards them — this can add at most a spurious lick to the last trial of a session. The delivered class fractions are within tolerance of the reference (fraction error 0.037).\n\nii.\n```python\n        lf=lick[np.isfinite(lick)] if lick.size else lick\n        if lf.size:\n            lt=np.interp(lf,np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0; lb=(lt/BIN_S).astype(int); lb=lb[(lb>=0)&(lb<nb)]; L[np.unique(lb)]=1\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Any lick in a temporal bin -> binary licking.\" Step 22: the 1 s temporal binning was chosen specifically because \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed\".\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Through the same trial origin `t0` and the same 1 s bin grid of length `nb` as the neural array, with licks assigned to bins by their interpolated time. Because the window is the contiguous corridor traversal, only licks that fall inside the trial's temporal span are kept.\n\nii.\n```python\n        L=np.zeros(nb,np.int16)\n        ...\n            lt=np.interp(lf,np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0; lb=(lt/BIN_S).astype(int); lb=lb[(lb>=0)&(lb<nb)]; L[np.unique(lb)]=1\n        out=np.vstack([cat,L,pcat,vcat]).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii.
