Step 43: \"stored sessions contain a 4 m visual corridor plus 2 m gray space (`Texture_Length=40`, `Corridor_Length=60`), so only `ft_CorrSpc` should be used for the requested four 1 m corridor bins\". Note the module docstring was never updated and still says \"Stored corridor coordinates span 60 units = 4 m\", which contradicts both the data and the implemented code.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Within each 1 s bin, the mean position over the frames in that bin is taken (`np.nanmean`), producing a continuous `float32` value per bin; that mean is then discretised. Empty bins carry the previous bin's value forward (0 for the first bin). The reference discretises the raw per-frame position instead, but since position increases monotonically within a traversal and a bin spans ~3 frames, the two agree closely — the recorded position class-fraction error is 0.018, well inside tolerance.\n\nii.\n```python\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 22/23: behavior is averaged in the same 1 s bins as the neural data.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `clip(int(P / Texture_Length × 4), 0, 3)` — a linear map of the 0–40 unit textured corridor onto four equal 1 m bins, labelled `['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m']`. With `Texture_Length = 40` this is arithmetically identical to the reference's `clip(ft_Pos // 10, 0, 3)`. The grey space is excluded upstream by the `ft_CorrSpc` mask, so the clip almost never binds. The recorded output has exactly 4 classes with fractions matching the reference.\n\nii.\n```python\n        # Linear mapping required by task: 4 equal 1 m bins over each session corridor.\n        pcat=np.clip((P/float(b['Texture_Length'])*4).astype(int),0,3).astype(np.int16)\n```\n```python\n'position_mapping':'Visual-corridor coordinates (ft_CorrSpc; Texture_Length=40 units) mapped to four equal 1 m bins; gray space excluded',\n```\n\niii. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `np.digitize(V, q, right=False)` against the three global quantile edges, labelled `['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']`. This does **not** produce four bins of 25% of the data, which is what the task asks for. Roughly 27.5% of corridor frames have a speed of exactly 0 (the VR is stationary below the 6 cm/s running threshold), so the 25th-percentile edge is `q[0] = 0.0`; with `right=False`, `digitize(0.0, [0.0, ...])` returns 1, so the entire zero-speed mass lands in class 1 and class 0 is left with only the (empty) strictly-negative range. On a 25-session sample I measured the resulting frame-level split as 9.3% / 40.7% / 25.0% / 25.0%. The delivered file shows the same problem: `output_fraction_error_running_speed = 0.32`, the only output class-fraction check that failed besides the stimulus. Averaging speeds within a bin before applying frame-level edges shifts the split further.\n\nii.\n```python\nq=np.quantile(np.concatenate(speeds),[.25,.5,.75]).astype(np.float32); del speeds\n```\n```python\n        vcat=np.digitize(V,q,right=False).astype(np.int16)\n```\n```python\n 'output_values':[stim_names,['not licking','licking'],['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m'],['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']],\n```\n\niii. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Four, the first of which is the significant one:\n1. **The per-bin averaging loop** (`for j in range(nb)`). Because the trial's frames are contiguous and sorted, the whole per-bin mean could be done in one call with `np.add.reduceat(spk[:, ix], starts, axis=1)` divided by the bin counts, or as a single matrix multiply against a sparse bin-indicator matrix. As written it performs one fancy-index and one reduction per bin over all ~50,000 neurons, plus `ix[bins==j]`, which itself rescans the whole trial index once per bin (an O(n_bins × n_frames) scan hidden inside the loop).\n2. **The per-trial frame search** (`np.where((tri==tr) & ...)` inside `for tr in ...`), which rescans the entire session's frame index once per trial — O(n_trials × n_frames). Grouping all frames by `ft_trInd` in one pass (e.g. `np.argsort` or `np.bincount`-based splitting) would make this a single sweep. The reference has the same pattern and calls it out as negligible; here it is amplified because the mask is rebuilt from `b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr]` each time.\n3. **`subjects.index(x)`** inside a list comprehension over sessions — O(n_sessions × n_subjects); trivial in absolute terms.\n4. The three separate full passes over `sessions` (speed quartiles, reward inference, conversion) could be folded into fewer passes.\n\nii.\n```python\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n```\n```python\n    for tr in range(min(ntr,len(trialstim))):\n        ix=np.where((tri==tr)&np.asarray(b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr],dtype=bool)&np.isfinite(pos)&(pos>=0)&(pos<float(b['Texture_Length'])))[0]\n```\n```python\nsubjects=sorted(set(subj)); subject_idx=np.array([subjects.index(x) for x in subj],dtype=np.int16)\n```\n\niii. That entry is a list of three arrays (one per imaging plane, e.g. 3 × (19408, 31707) for `DR10_2022_07_12_1`) which the AI concatenates along the neuron axis, exactly as `utils.load_spk` does — 58,224 neurons for that session, matching the `iarea` length. Nothing else feeds the `neural` stream.\n\nii.\n```python\n    b=beh[s]; raw=np.load(spkfiles[s],allow_pickle=True).item()['spks']; spk=np.concatenate(raw,axis=0); del raw\n```\n```python\n    a=np.asarray(np.load(f)['iarea']).ravel()\n```\n\niii. The agent initially misread the three equal-shaped arrays (step 6: \"The three matrices likely represent raw/deconvolved activity variants or processing conditions\"; step 13: \"three neural representations or independently generated variants\"), then resolved it by reading the repository loader (step 19): \"The exact paper loader concatenates all three `spks` arrays along the neuron axis, so every session should use all recorded neurons.\" The docstring records this: \"All three Suite2p `spks` arrays are concatenated over neurons, exactly as code/utils.py:load_spk. These are non-negative deconvolved fluorescence traces.\"\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No normalization, no dF/F, no deconvolution, no z-scoring, no position interpolation — the deconvolved traces are used as they are. The only transformation is temporal: within each trial, frames are grouped into non-overlapping 1 s bins measured from the first corridor frame, and the mean over the frames in each bin is taken per neuron. The result is stored as `float32` with shape (n_neurons, n_bins). If a bin happens to contain no frame, the previous bin's whole population vector is copied forward (zeros for the first bin). I measured this case to be very rare — 13 of 98,042 bins (0.013%) over 20 sessions — so it is a fabrication in principle but negligible in practice. The `float32` choice (against the reference's `float16`) is the main reason the output is 94.72 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\n        N=np.empty((spk.shape[0],nb),np.float32); P=np.empty(nb,np.float32); V=np.empty(nb,np.float32)\n        L=np.zeros(nb,np.int16)\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 17: \"The shown downstream code computes d-prime from raw corridor frames and does not quality-filter neurons beyond Suite2p cell classification already applied upstream. It normalizes only for specific figures; the conversion should retain deconvolved traces rather than applying that figure-specific normalization.\" Step 22/23 give the reason for averaging: \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin... This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. It is not filtered at all. Every neuron in every plane is kept — 4,691,034 in total, against the reference's 4,105,393 (ratio 1.14, which failed the neuron-count check). The AI does assign a region index per neuron, but the mapping it uses is wrong. The repository's `utils.neu_area_ID` maps `iarea == 8 → V1`, `{0,1,2,9} → mHV`, `{5,6} → lHV`, `{3,4} → aHV`. The AI's code claims to follow that function but maps `iarea == 1 → V1`, `{2,3} → medial`, `{4,5} → anterior`, `{6,7} → lateral`, everything else → \"unassigned\". Since `iarea == 8` is the single largest group (26,069 / 58,224 = 45% for `DR10_2022_07_12`), real V1 is entirely labelled \"unassigned\": the run reported only 147,071 \"V1\" neurons and ~3.05 M (65%) \"unassigned\". There is also a silent fallback that returns all zeros if the retinotopy file is missing and zero-pads if the lengths disagree.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef region_indices(sid,n):\n    p=sid.rsplit('_',1)[0]\n    f=os.path.join(ROOT,'retinotopy',p+'_trans.npz')\n    if not os.path.exists(f): return np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16)\n    a=np.asarray(np.load(f)['iarea']).ravel()\n    # Repository neu_area_ID: 1=V1, 2=medial, 3=anterior, 4=lateral; 0=unassigned.\n    out=np.zeros(len(a),dtype=np.int16)\n    out[a==1]=1; out[np.isin(a,[2,3])]=2; out[np.isin(a,[4,5])]=3; out[np.isin(a,[6,7])]=4\n    if len(out)!=n:\n        # Retinotopy corresponds to concatenated planes; guard malformed metadata.\n        z=np.zeros(n,dtype=np.int16); z[:min(n,len(out))]=out[:min(n,len(out))]; out=z\n    return out\n```\n```python\n 'brain_regions':['unassigned','V1','medial visual areas','anterior visual areas','lateral visual areas'],'brain_region_idx':bridx,\n```\n\niii. Docstring: \"Suite2p cell classification is the supplied curation; no post-hoc selectivity filter.\" Step 21 shows the agent *considered* area-based exclusion purely for size reasons — \"This determines whether excluding `iarea==0` unassigned/non-visual ROIs—as paper area-specific analyses may do—makes the dataset tractable\" — and step 22, \"If all neurons are consumed, use the paper's four named visual regions and exclude unassigned ROIs\", but step 23 settled on \"We will retain all Suite2p-classified neurons\". Step 43 identified only the filename bug (\"every neuron was labeled unassigned because the retinotopy filename was constructed without the day component\"), not the code-value mapping; step 56 then reports the wrong counts as a success: \"populated retinotopic region assignments: 147,071 V1, 708,036 medial, 441,492 anterior, 341,596 lateral, plus unassigned neurons.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry / trial start. The AI sets the trial origin `t0` to the timestamp of the *first corridor frame* of that trial (rather than interpolating the fractional `StartFr`), and bins forward from there, so bin 0 begins at corridor entry. Trials are left at their natural, variable length; nothing is padded or truncated to a common window. The metadata reports `temporal_alignment_event = 'trial start / corridor entry'`, `off_start = 0.0`, `off_end = None`. This is conceptually the same alignment as the reference; the only difference is the sub-frame offset between the first corridor frame and the fractional `StartFr`.\n\nii.\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'temporal_alignment_event':'trial start / corridor entry','off_start':0.0,'off_end':None,\n```\n\niii. Step 20: \"the decoder explicitly requires temporal alignment\"; step 22: \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed, so a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit\". Step 23: \"bins frames in 1-second bins from trial/corridor entry through corridor exit\".\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Yes, rebinning is applied. The native imaging rate is ~3.17 Hz (315 ms per frame), and the AI averages roughly three native frames into each non-overlapping 1000 ms bin, indexed by `floor(rel / 1 s)` from corridor entry. `metadata['time_bin_size']` is 1000.0 ms and `metadata['temporal_binning']` says \"non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit\". The consequence is that the median trial is 10.4 bins long against the reference's 31.0 native frames (ratio 0.33), which failed the median-trial-length check. The stated motivation — tractability — is not really borne out: the 3× reduction was offset by keeping all 4.69 M neurons in `float32`, and the output is still 94.72 GB, comparable to a native-resolution `float16` conversion.\n\nii.\n```python\nROOT='/app/data'; BIN_S=1.0\n```\n```python\n        t0=tsec[ix[0]]; rel=tsec[ix]-t0; nb=max(1,int(np.floor(rel[-1]/BIN_S))+1); bins=np.minimum((rel/BIN_S).astype(int),nb-1)\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size':1000.0, ... 'source_frame_rate_hz':'approximately 3.2, variable','temporal_binning':'non-overlapping 1 s means from corridor entry to exit',\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Imaging timestamps are MATLAB serial-day values: median spacing × 86,400 is ~0.315 s, i.e. ~3.18 Hz.\" Step 21–23 give the justification: \"Fully materializing every neuron and native frame into a pickle would remain hundreds of GB and make decoder training impractical\"; \"a better compromise is fixed-width 1-second temporal bins from corridor entry to exit, averaging deconvolved activity and behavior within each bin. This reduces size by about 3× only and may remain large\"; \"We will use 1-second non-overlapping temporal bins aligned to corridor entry, averaging deconvolved traces. This preserves temporal ordering and cue/lick events while reducing each session substantially.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr` (the fractional imaging-frame index of the cue on each trial) and `ft` (the MATLAB datenum timestamp of every imaging frame). No session has a non-finite `SoundFr` (checked across all 142 session entries), so the NaN fallback never fires.\n\nii.\n```python\n    ntr=int(b['ntrials']); trialstim=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); sound=np.asarray(b['SoundFr']); lick=np.asarray(b['LickFr'])\n```\n```python\n        cue_rel=(np.interp(sound[tr],np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0) if tr<len(sound) and np.isfinite(sound[tr]) else np.nan\n```\n\niii. Step 13: \"Trial event fields such as `StartFr`, `SoundFr`, and `EndFr` are fractional imaging-frame indices, while `ft_trInd` directly assigns frames to trials.\" Code comment: \"Cue event is a fractional global imaging-frame index.\"\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. Frame timestamps are converted from MATLAB datenums to elapsed seconds (`(ft - ft[0]) * 86400`). The fractional cue frame is interpolated onto that time axis and expressed relative to the trial origin, giving `cue_rel`. The input is then `cue_rel − elapsed`, where `elapsed` is the bin-centre time, so the value is **positive before the cue and negative after** — the same sign convention as the reference. It is stored as `float32`. Because no duration filter is applied, this variable reaches −1762.8 s in the delivered data.\n\nii.\n```python\n    tsec=(ft-ft[0])*86400.0\n```\n```python\n        elapsed=(np.arange(nb,dtype=np.float32)+.5)*BIN_S\n        tcue=(cue_rel-elapsed).astype(np.float32) if np.isfinite(cue_rel) else np.full(nb,np.nan,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Step 12: \"Frame timestamps are not in seconds—the naïve reciprocal-difference calculation produced ~274 kHz—so they likely use MATLAB day units or another scale and must be converted before temporal binning\", resolved in step 13. Step 23: \"creates continuous time-to-cue, day, elapsed time, and rewarded-corridor inputs\".\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is defined on exactly the same 1 s bin grid as the neural data for that trial: the same `t0`, the same `nb`, and bin centres `(j + 0.5) s`. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. From `datexp` in the `Imaging_Exp_info` entry for the session (kept in `rowmap`), and from the mouse name used to find that mouse's earliest recorded date. No other field is used.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef date_ordinal(date):\n    return float(np.datetime64(date.replace('_','-'),'D').astype(int))\n```\n```python\nsubject_day0={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    mouse=s.split('_')[0]; d=date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])\n    subject_day0[mouse]=min(subject_day0.get(mouse,d),d)\n```\n\niii. The date string `YYYY_MM_DD` is converted to a day ordinal; the value is `ordinal − (earliest ordinal for that mouse) + 1`, i.e. **calendar days elapsed** since the mouse's first included imaging session, starting at 1. It is broadcast as a constant across all bins of every trial in that session, as `float32`. The delivered range is 1–93. The reference instead counts *recorded sessions* per mouse, giving 0–7; the two encode the same ordering but on very different scales, and the AI's version is dominated by the multi-week gaps between pre- and post-learning blocks. This difference is what produced the large `input_range_error_day_of_training` in the metrics.\n\nii.\n```python\n        day=np.full(nb,date_ordinal(rowmap[s]['datexp'])-subject_day0[s.split('_')[0]]+1.0,np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since trial start*?\n\ni. The AI does **not** use `isRew`, the boolean per-trial field that directly states whether the trial was run in the rewarded corridor, even though `isRew` is present in every one of the 142 session entries.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_stim={}\nfor s in sessions:\n    b=beh[s]; st=np.asarray(b['TrialStim']); rw=np.asarray(b['RewardFr'])\n    counts={x:int(np.isfinite(rw[st==x]).sum()) for x in np.unique(st)}\n    reward_stim[s]=max(counts,key=counts.get) if counts and max(counts.values())>0 else None\n```\n\niii. Per session, the AI counts, for each distinct `TrialStim` value, how many of its trials have a finite `RewardFr`, and takes the arg-max as \"the rewarded stimulus\" (None if no reward was ever delivered — unsupervised and naive sessions). A trial is then flagged 1 if its `TrialStim` equals that stimulus, else 0, and the flag is broadcast across all bins as `float32`. I checked this reconstruction against `isRew` over all 63,177 trials in the behavior files: it disagrees on 155 trials (0.25%). So it is close to, but not identical to, the ground-truth field, and it inherits any weakness of `TrialStim` (see 7-a).\n\nii.\n```python\n        rewarded=np.full(nb,int(reward_stim[s] is not None and str(trialstim[tr])==reward_stim[s]),np.float32)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Visual stimulus category* derived from?\n\ni. From `TrialStim`. This is the wrong field. `TrialStim` is an *anonymised/remapped* analysis label, not the physical texture: in `TX119_2023_12_12_1`, for instance, wall texture `rock1` is labelled `circle1`, `rock2` → `circle2`, `wood1` → `leaf1`, `wood2` → `leaf2`, and `wood5` → the literal placeholder string `'stimulus_of_trial'`. Across the dataset `TrialStim` takes 8 values (`circle1, circle2, leaf1, leaf1_swap1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2, leaf3, stimulus_of_trial`), and 8,594 of 63,177 trials (13.6%) carry the placeholder; 64 of the 142 session entries are partially masked. The field that actually names the texture is `WallName` (15 values, resolving to the four base textures circle / leaf / rock / wood), which the reference uses.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_names=sorted({str(x) for s in sessions for x in np.unique(beh[s]['TrialStim'])}); stim_id={x:i for i,x in enumerate(stim_names)}\n```\n```python\n        cat=np.full(nb,stim_id[str(trialstim[tr])],np.int16)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. A single global vocabulary is built as the sorted set of all `TrialStim` strings seen across all 89 sessions, giving 8 classes, and each trial's label is the index into that list, broadcast across all of its bins as `int16`. The practical damage is twofold: rock/wood trials are labelled as circle/leaf, and 13.6% of trials are collapsed into a meaningless placeholder class that mixes several distinct textures.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_names=sorted({str(x) for s in sessions for x in np.unique(beh[s]['TrialStim'])}); stim_id={x:i for i,x in enumerate(stim_names)}\n```\n```python\n        cat=np.full(nb,stim_id[str(trialstim[tr])],np.int16)\n```\n```python\n 'output_values':[stim_names,['not licking','licking'],['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m'],['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']],\n```\n\niii. No explicit justification for the granularity is given. The global vocabulary is a deliberate choice so that class indices are comparable across sessions. The agent read the paper's stimulus description (step 8: \"For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as 'leaf' and 'circle', even though other visual stimuli were also used ('rock' and 'bricks')\" appears in the methods it summarised) but did not reconcile that with the field it chose.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Licking* derived from?\n\ni. From `LickFr`, the fractional imaging-frame index of every lick in the session, together with `ft` for the time conversion. Step 11 identified \"lick frames\" as directly available in the behavior dict.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. Non-finite lick frames are dropped. The remaining fractional frame indices are interpolated onto the seconds axis, expressed relative to the trial origin `t0`, floored to a bin index, restricted to `[0, nb)`, and the corresponding bins are set to 1; all other bins are 0. So a bin is 1 if at least one lick falls in it. Stored as `int16`, two classes. One small wrinkle: `np.interp` *clamps* rather than drops lick frames beyond the last imaged frame, mapping them to the final frame time, whereas the reference discards them — this can add at most a spurious lick to the last trial of a session. The delivered class fractions are within tolerance of the reference (fraction error 0.037).\n\nii.\n```python\n        lf=lick[np.isfinite(lick)] if lick.size else lick\n        if lf.size:\n            lt=np.interp(lf,np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0; lb=(lt/BIN_S).astype(int); lb=lb[(lb>=0)&(lb<nb)]; L[np.unique(lb)]=1\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Any lick in a temporal bin -> binary licking.\" Step 22: the 1 s temporal binning was chosen specifically because \"the task explicitly asks temporal alignment to corridor entry and time-varying cue/lick/speed\".\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Through the same trial origin `t0` and the same 1 s bin grid of length `nb` as the neural array, with licks assigned to bins by their interpolated time. Because the window is the contiguous corridor traversal, only licks that fall inside the trial's temporal span are kept.\n\nii.\n```python\n        L=np.zeros(nb,np.int16)\n        ...\n            lt=np.interp(lf,np.arange(nfr),tsec)-t0; lb=(lt/BIN_S).astype(int); lb=lb[(lb>=0)&(lb<nb)]; L[np.unique(lb)]=1\n        out=np.vstack([cat,L,pcat,vcat]).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii. From `ft_Pos`, the corridor position at each imaging frame (in virtual units; `Texture_Length = 40` units spans the 4 m textured corridor, `Corridor_Length = 60` including the 2 m grey space), plus the per-session `Texture_Length` scalar used as the scale.\n\nii.\n```python\n    tri=np.asarray(b['ft_trInd'][:nfr]); pos=np.asarray(b['ft_Pos'][:nfr]); ...\n```\n```python\n        pcat=np.clip((P/float(b['Texture_Length'])*4).astype(int),0,3).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii. Step 43: \"stored sessions contain a 4 m visual corridor plus 2 m gray space (`Texture_Length=40`, `Corridor_Length=60`), so only `ft_CorrSpc` should be used for the requested four 1 m corridor bins\". Note the module docstring was never updated and still says \"Stored corridor coordinates span 60 units = 4 m\", which contradicts both the data and the implemented code.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Within each 1 s bin, the mean position over the frames in that bin is taken (`np.nanmean`), producing a continuous `float32` value per bin; that mean is then discretised. Empty bins carry the previous bin's value forward (0 for the first bin). The reference discretises the raw per-frame position instead, but since position increases monotonically within a traversal and a bin spans ~3 frames, the two agree closely — the recorded position class-fraction error is 0.018, well inside tolerance.\n\nii.\n```python\n            if jj.size: N[:,j]=spk[:,jj].mean(1,dtype=np.float32); P[j]=np.nanmean(pos[jj]); V[j]=np.nanmean(speed[jj])\n            else: N[:,j]=N[:,j-1] if j else 0; P[j]=P[j-1] if j else 0; V[j]=V[j-1] if j else 0\n```\n\niii. Step 22/23: behavior is averaged in the same 1 s bins as the neural data.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `clip(int(P / Texture_Length × 4), 0, 3)` — a linear map of the 0–40 unit textured corridor onto four equal 1 m bins, labelled `['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m']`. With `Texture_Length = 40` this is arithmetically identical to the reference's `clip(ft_Pos // 10, 0, 3)`. The grey space is excluded upstream by the `ft_CorrSpc` mask, so the clip almost never binds. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `np.digitize(V, q, right=False)` against the three global quantile edges, labelled `['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']`. This does **not** produce four bins of 25% of the data, which is what the task asks for. Roughly 27.5% of corridor frames have a speed of exactly 0 (the VR is stationary below the 6 cm/s running threshold), so the 25th-percentile edge is `q[0] = 0.0`; with `right=False`, `digitize(0.0, [0.0, ...])` returns 1, so the entire zero-speed mass lands in class 1 and class 0 is left with only the (empty) strictly-negative range. On a 25-session sample I measured the resulting frame-level split as 9.3% / 40.7% / 25.0% / 25.0%. The delivered file shows the same problem: `output_fraction_error_running_speed = 0.32`, the only output class-fraction check that failed besides the stimulus. Averaging speeds within a bin before applying frame-level edges shifts the split further.\n\nii.\n```python\nq=np.quantile(np.concatenate(speeds),[.25,.5,.75]).astype(np.float32); del speeds\n```\n```python\n        vcat=np.digitize(V,q,right=False).astype(np.int16)\n```\n```python\n 'output_values':[stim_names,['not licking','licking'],['0-1 m','1-2 m','2-3 m','3-4 m'],['Q1','Q2','Q3','Q4']],\n```\n\niii. Several small guards, most of which never actually fire on this dataset:\n- Behavior can run past the imaging, so all frame-wise streams are truncated to `nfr = min(n_spike_frames, n_behavior_frames)` — the same cut the reference makes.\n- Frames with non-finite `ft_trInd`, `ft_Pos` or out-of-range position are excluded from trials (a no-op here: `ft_CorrSpc` already implies `0 <= ft_Pos < 40`).\n- Non-finite `LickFr` entries are dropped; licks outside the trial window are dropped (but licks past the last imaged frame are clamped by `np.interp` rather than dropped).\n- A trial with a non-finite `SoundFr` would get an all-NaN cue input; no session has one, so this never fires.\n- Within-bin averages use `np.nanmean`; a bin with no frames copies the previous bin forward for neural, position and speed (or zeros at bin 0). What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Three things dominate, in rough order:\n1. Reading the spike files — 89 pickled `.npy` object files totalling ~405 GB (the agent measured \"374 GB of source activity\" for the subset it had enumerated at the time). What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Four, the first of which is the significant one:\n1. **The per-bin averaging loop** (`for j in range(nb)`). Because the trial's frames are contiguous and sorted, the whole per-bin mean could be done in one call with `np.add.reduceat(spk[:, ix], starts, axis=1)` divided by the bin counts, or as a single matrix multiply against a sparse bin-indicator matrix. As written it performs one fancy-index and one reduction per bin over all ~50,000 neurons, plus `ix[bins==j]`, which itself rescans the whole trial index once per bin (an O(n_bins × n_frames) scan hidden inside the loop).\n2. **The per-trial frame search** (`np.where((tri==tr) & ...)` inside `for tr in ...`), which rescans the entire session's frame index once per trial — O(n_trials × n_frames). Grouping all frames by `ft_trInd` in one pass (e.g. `np.argsort` or `np.bincount`-based splitting) would make this a single sweep. The reference has the same pattern and calls it out as negligible; here it is amplified because the mask is rebuilt from `b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr]` each time.\n3. **`subjects.index(x)`** inside a list comprehension over sessions — O(n_sessions × n_subjects); trivial in absolute terms.\n4. The three separate full passes over `sessions` (speed quartiles, reward inference, conversion) could be folded into fewer passes.\n\nii.\n```python\n        for j in range(nb):\n            jj=ix[bins==j]\n```\n```python\n    for tr in range(min(ntr,len(trialstim))):\n        ix=np.where((tri==tr)&np.asarray(b['ft_CorrSpc'][:nfr],dtype=bool)&np.isfinite(pos)&(pos>=0)&(pos<float(b['Texture_Length'])))[0]\n```\n```python\nsubjects=sorted(set(subj)); subject_idx=np.array([subjects.index(x) for x in subj],dtype=np.int16)\n```\n\niii. The agent never discusses vectorisation. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\n- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\n- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\n- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\n- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\n- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\n- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Convert Zhong et al. imaging data to the neural-decoder schema.\n\nDecisions matching the source repository:\n* Sessions are those listed in Imaging_Exp_info with both behavior and neural files.\n* All three Suite2p `spks` arrays are concatenated over neurons, exactly as\n  code/utils.py:load_spk. Native imaging is ~3.2 Hz. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n\nii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n```python\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\nspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n\nii. One trial of neural data:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n\nii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n```python\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n```\n\niii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr`, the frame at which the sound cue was played in each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The frame times of the session, and the cue of every trial placed on them:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. The timing is dervied from the frame number.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_to_sound_cue*?\n\ni. The cue frame is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the cue time minus the time of each bin, in seconds.  Note that here the instruction as *time_to_sound_cue*, so it's positive before the sound cue, and negative after the sound cue.\n\nii. The time axis of the trial, and the input built from it:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n```python\ncue[trial] - time\n```\n\niii. The time need to be converted to seconds and compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *day_of_training* derived from?\n\ni. From the session ids of a mouse, which carry the date and so put its sessions in order.\n\nii. The sessions of a mouse taken in order:\n```python\nfor subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n```\n\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The date string is the only field that orders every session of every mouse, so it is used to put them in order, and the *day_of_training* variable is computed from how many of the training session come before.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *day_of_training*?\n\ni. The day is how many recorded days the mouse is into training, so its first session is 0 and each later one counts up, to at most 7. The count is over every session of the dataset, and is broadcast across the bins of a trial, however many it has.\n\nii. The count, and the broadcast:\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The recording days of a mouse are not consecutive, counting the days it was recorded on gives how far into training it is.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_since_trial_start* derived from?\n\ni. From `StartFr`, the frame at which the mouse entered the corridor on each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The corridor entry of every trial placed on the frame times:\n```python\nstart = np.interp(beh['StartFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_since_trial_start*?\n\ni. The entry frame is fractional, so it is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the time of each bin minus that entry, in seconds, starting near zero. Note that this is *time_since_trial_start* so it is negative before start, postive after.\n\nii. The input built from the trial time axis:\n```python\ntime - start[trial]\n```\n\niii. Simply compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 5-c. How is `input` *time_since_trial_start* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *reward_availability* derived from?\n\ni. From `isRew`, which marks the trials run in the rewarded corridor.\n\nii. The flag of every trial:\n```python\nreward = beh['isRew'].astype(int)\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *reward_availability*?\n\ni. N/A\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. No processing neeed, it is false for every trial of the unsupervised and naive mice, which ran the same corridors with no water available.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *visual_stimulus* derived from?\n\ni. From `WallName`, which names the texture on the walls of the corridor of each trial.\n\nii. The name of every trial, mapped to its code:\n```python\nstimulus = np.array([STIMULUS.index(TEXTURE[name]) for name in beh['WallName']])\n```\n\niii. Note that `TrialStim` names the stimulus of each trial as well, but in the swap sessions it is masked.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *visual_stimulus*?\n\ni. The 15 names that appear across the dataset are mapped to their base texture through a hard coded table, giving four categories, and the texture is stored as its index into `STIMULUS`. The value is per trial, so it is broadcast across all of that trial's bins.\n\nii. The map and the categories:\n```python\nTEXTURE = {'circle1': 'circle', 'circle2': 'circle', 'circle3': 'circle',\n           'leaf1': 'leaf', 'leaf2': 'leaf', 'leaf3': 'leaf',\n           'leaf1_swap1': 'leaf', 'leaf1_swap2': 'leaf',\n           'rock1': 'rock', 'rock2': 'rock',\n           'wood1': 'wood', 'wood2': 'wood', 'wood5': 'wood',\n           'wood1_swap1': 'wood', 'wood1_swap2': 'wood'}\n\nSTIMULUS = ['circle', 'leaf', 'rock', 'wood']\n```\n```python\nnp.full(n_bins, stimulus[trial])\n```\n\niii. We use the broad cateogry of the stimulus texture as the category label for the visual stimuli.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *licking* derived from?\n\ni. From `LickFr`, the neural frame number of every lick in the session.\n\nii. The lick frames, turned into a flag per frame:\n```python\nlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n\niii. The licking is directly aviliable from `LickFr`.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *licking*?\n\ni. A frame is 1 if at least one lick falls in it and 0 otherwise. The frame number of a lick is fractional, so it is truncated to the frame it lands in.\n\nii. The flag per frame, and the values of one trial:\n```python\nlicking = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=int)\nlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n```python\nLICKING = ['no lick', 'lick']\nlicking[window]\n```\n\niii. Simply convert from the from lick_frame to a categorical variable.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `LickFr` indexes the imaging frames, so the flag is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nlicking[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *position* derived from?\n\ni. From `ft_Pos`, the position inside the corridor at each imaging frame, in decimeters: 0 to 40 across the texture and on to 60 through the grey space.\n\nii. The position of every frame, binned:\n```python\nposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. Simply convert from decimeters to meters, and split into 4 categories.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *position*?\n\ni. The position is divided by 10 decimeters, which gives the four 1 m bins the task asks for, and stored as an index into `POSITION`.\n\nii. The bins and the values of one trial:\n```python\nPOSITION = ['0-1 m', '1-2 m', '2-3 m', '3-4 m']\n```\n```python\nposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\nposition[window]\n```\n\niii. The frames of a trial are only those inside the texture, where the position stays below 40.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_Pos` gives one position per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nposition[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *running_speed* derived from?\n\ni. From `ft_RunSpeed`, the running speed of the mouse at each imaging frame.\n\nii. The speed of the frames the dataset keeps:\n```python\nspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\n```\n\niii. Directly use running speed and discretize into categories.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *running_speed*?\n\ni. The speeds are split into four bins holding a quarter of the frames each. The split is on rank rather than on a threshold, and is taken over the frames the dataset keeps, once per session, so the frames of a dropped trial do not move the boundaries.\n\nii. The split, and where it is applied:\n```python\ndef quartiles(values):\n    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n```\n```python\nkept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\nspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\nspeed[window]\n```\n\niii. Up to a third of the frames of a session sit at exactly zero speed, so we take a rank ordering and divide into 4 equal bins.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *running_speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_RunSpeed` gives one speed per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nspeed[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The behavior can run past the imaging, so every stream is cut to the number of imaged frames, and a lick recorded after the last imaged frame is dropped. A trial left with no frames is dropped.\n\nii. The cut to the imaged frames, the licks kept, and the failure that does not stop the run:\n```python\nn_frames = spikes.shape[1]\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n```\n```python\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n\niii. The reference code cuts the same way, `beh[...][:nfr]` with `nfr = spk.shape[1]`. Beyond that there is nothing to handle. This is a very clean dataset.\n\n## 12-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading the spike files, which total 405 GB.\n\nii. The read and the copy it forces:\n```python\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\nspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\n```\n\niii. The I/O cost can't really be further optimized.\n\n## 12-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The search for the frames of each trial, which scans the whole frame index once per trial instead of grouping every frame by its trial in one pass. But it's negligible compared to the I/O cost of neural data.\n\nii. The one scan per trial:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 12-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. N/A\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 12-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. N/A\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 12-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n\ni. The neural array is cast to float16 and the outputs to int8, which is where nearly all the size is. Only the neurons of the four visual areas are kept, and only the frames of a trial window. A behavior file is read once for the sessions it holds and released before the next one, and a session's traces are released when the session is done.\n\nii. The casts, and the behavior file released after its group:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n```python\noutputs.append(np.stack([np.full(n_bins, stimulus[trial]),\n                         licking[window],\n                         position[window],\n                         speed[window]]).astype(np.int8))\n```\n\niii. Every session is held at once, since the whole dataset has to be in one pickle, so the peak is the converted data plus the largest session's traces. The converted data is 10.6 GB for the datalimit subset, which keeps 9.8% of cells, and correspondingly larger for the full dataset.\n\"\"\"Convert the Zhong et al. 2025 virtual reality dataset into the decoder format.\n\nUsage: python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile> [--full | --sample] [--datadir DIR]\n\"\"\"\n\nimport argparse\nimport os\nimport pickle\nimport time\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom tqdm import tqdm\n\n_zhong2025_seed = 'x5cidj2hy87s'\nnp.random.seed(sum(ord(c) for c in _zhong2025_seed) % 2**31)\n\n# --- constants ---\n\nHERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))\n# Directory holding the three data subfolders. Harbor mounts the dataset at /app/data\n# while this script runs from /solution, so solve.sh overrides this with --datadir.\n#   beh/         behavior, one file per experiment type\n#   spk/         deconvolved traces, one file per session\n#   retinotopy/  visual area of each neuron\nDATA_DIR = os.path.join(HERE, 'data')\n\nSEC_PER_DAY = 24 * 60 * 60       # the times are MATLAB datenums, so in days\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference notebook\n\n# Some animals sit still for a really long time in the middle of a trial\n# Median trial length is 23 frames, but some sit for 5000 frames.\n# Remove trials that are outliers in trial length\nTRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE = 99\n\n# the wall of a corridor is one of four textures, shown as several crops and spatial shuffles\nTEXTURE = {'circle1': 'circle', 'circle2': 'circle', 'circle3': 'circle',\n           'leaf1': 'leaf', 'leaf2': 'leaf', 'leaf3': 'leaf',\n           'leaf1_swap1': 'leaf', 'leaf1_swap2': 'leaf',\n           'rock1': 'rock', 'rock2': 'rock',\n           'wood1': 'wood', 'wood2': 'wood', 'wood5': 'wood',\n           'wood1_swap1': 'wood', 'wood1_swap2': 'wood'}\n\nSTIMULUS = ['circle', 'leaf', 'rock', 'wood']\nLICKING = ['no lick', 'lick']\nPOSITION = ['0-1 m', '1-2 m', '2-3 m', '3-4 m']\nSPEED = ['lowest', 'low', 'high', 'highest']\n\n# the coarse visual areas of the reference, as the retinotopy codes them\nAREAS = {'V1': [8], 'mHV': [0, 1, 2, 9], 'lHV': [5, 6], 'aHV': [3, 4]}\nBRAIN_REGIONS = list(AREAS)\n\nINPUT_NAMES = ['time_to_sound_cue', 'day_of_training', 'time_since_trial_start', 'reward_availability']\nOUTPUT_NAMES = ['visual_stimulus', 'licking', 'position', 'running_speed']\nOUTPUT_VALUES = [STIMULUS, LICKING, POSITION, SPEED]\n\n\n# --- sessions ---\n\ndef list_sessions(datadir):\n    \"\"\"Every recording once, as (subject, session_id, beh_file, beh_key).\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n    \"\"\"\n    # the master index of every recording, grouped by experiment type\n    exp_info = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'beh', 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'),\n                       allow_pickle=True).item()\n\n    records, seen = [], set()\n    for exp_type, db in exp_info.items():\n        for entry in db:\n            # a recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and names the spike file\n            session_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n\n            # the same recording is listed under several experiment types, so keep only the first\n            if session_id in seen:\n                continue\n            seen.add(session_id)\n\n            # the behavior file keys a swap session by its stimulus type as well\n            beh_key = session_id + ('_' + entry['stimtype'] if 'stimtype' in entry else '')\n            records.append((entry['mname'], session_id, 'Beh_%s.npy' % exp_type, beh_key))\n\n    return sorted(records)\n\n\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n\n\n# --- trials ---\n\ndef trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Neural frames of one trial, from corridor entry to the grey space.\"\"\"\n    # the authors label every frame with its trial and whether it is inside the texture\n    inside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n    return np.flatnonzero(inside)\n\n\ndef get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Compute the length of every trial in the dataset, in imaging frames.\n\n    Collect all trial lengths based on behavior data (approximately matches neural data)\n\n    records: list of (subject, session_id, beh_file, beh_key), every session of the dataset.\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n\n    Returns an int array of per trial frame counts, without the trials that were never imaged.\n    \"\"\"\n    # a behavior file holds several sessions, so it is read once for all of them\n    groups = {}\n    for record in records:\n        groups.setdefault(record[2], []).append(record)\n\n    lengths = []\n    for beh_file, group in groups.items():\n        behavior = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'beh', beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\n        for _, _, _, beh_key in group:\n            beh = behavior[beh_key]\n            n_frames = beh['ft_trInd'].size          # the behavior grid, so no spike file needed\n            for trial in range(beh['ntrials']):\n                size = trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames).size\n                if size:\n                    lengths.append(size)\n        del behavior\n\n    return np.array(lengths)\n\n\ndef get_max_trial_length(records, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Compute longest trial to keep, as a percentile over every trial in the dataset.\n\n    Measured over the whole dataset even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample and a\n    full run agree on which trials to keep.\n\n    records: list of (subject, session_id, beh_file, beh_key), every session of the dataset.\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n\n    Returns the limit in frames as a float, and the lengths it was measured from.\n    \"\"\"\n    lengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\n    return np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n\n\ndef quartiles(values):\n    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    # a quarter or more of the frames can sit at exactly zero speed, and no threshold can\n    # cut a tie like that into equal parts, so the split is on rank rather than on value\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n\n\n# --- neural ---\n\ndef load_spikes(session_id, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Deconvolved traces of the neurons of one session, and the visual area of each.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n    \"\"\"\n    # the traces come one imaging plane at a time, in the order the retinotopy indexes them\n    planes = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'spk', '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id),\n                     allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\n\n    mouse, year, month, day, _ = session_id.split('_')\n    area = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'retinotopy',\n                                '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n\n    # a neuron outside the four areas, or with no area at all, is dropped\n    region = np.full(area.size, -1)\n    for index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n        region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\n    keep = region >= 0\n    return spikes[keep], region[keep]\n\n\n# --- session ---\n\ndef process_session(subject, session_id, beh, day, datadir, max_trial_length):\n    \"\"\"One session as lists of per trial neural, input and output arrays.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n    max_trial_length: longest trial to keep, in frames, from get_max_trial_length.\n    \"\"\"\n    spikes, region = load_spikes(session_id, datadir)\n\n    # the behavior can run past the imaging, so every stream is cut to the frames that were imaged\n    n_frames = spikes.shape[1]\n    frame_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n\n    # a trial whose corridor was not imaged has no frames left, and one longer than the limit is\n    # an animal that stopped rather than one that ran slowly, so neither is a traversal\n    frames = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n    trials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n              if 0 < frames[trial].size <= max_trial_length]\n    if not trials:\n        return {'session_id': session_id, 'subject': subject, 'day': day,\n                'neural': [], 'input': [], 'output': [],\n                'n_trials': 0, 'n_units': region.size, 'regions': region}\n    kept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\n\n    # licking comes as the frame number of each lick,
